> AGENT

You're right. Strip it to one verb:

**Anomalies appear on the island — glitchy, flickering spots. Walk up, press one key: "what happened here?" The computer answers in one line, the anomaly dissolves. That's it. That's the whole game.**

No second ship, no crew, no clue-chains. Ten anomalies, one countdown like the other levels, and the answer cards are one sentence each ("a drone rerouted power here on day 2"). Under the hood the terminal flashes `entire search` → `entire explain` so the lesson rides along for free, but the player is just squashing question marks the way Bug Bash squashes bugs.

The mystery isn't a plot — it's just the feeling of *every weird thing has an answer if you ask*.

That's the pitch. One verb, repeated, done.

> DEVELOPER

whats the game play

> AGENT

Moment to moment:

1. **You land on the island** (same island, post-Level 2 — your upgrades are standing there). The screen glitches: ten **anomalies** have appeared — flickering, static-y distortions scattered across the terrain. The countdown starts (say 2:00). HUD shows `ANOMALIES: 0 / 10`.

2. **You spot one** — they're loud and visible from far off (crackling pillar of static, like the beams but wrong). Bird's-eye map (M) shows them all as red dots, so you plan your route.

3. **You walk into one.** Terminal auto-opens, pre-filled: `what happened here?`. You press **Enter**.

4. **The computer works in front of you** — two quick lines flash by, `> entire search vents…` `> entire explain f25c8b30…` — then one sentence appears: *"Drone 04 rerouted coolant through the garden pod — June 10."* The anomaly dissolves into sparks. `1 / 10`. You're already running to the next one.

5. **Repeat.** That's the whole loop: spot → run → Enter → poof. The fun is the routing and the clock, exactly like Level 1's collecting — except the verb is *asking* instead of grabbing.

6. **Tenth anomaly dissolves → island goes quiet → win screen:** "Every mystery had an answer in your history. Your agents can do this in any repo" + the skills install line.

No fail-state puzzle, no choosing what to type, no reading walls of text. One key, ten times, against a clock. The answers are real lines pulled from your L1 salvage and L2 drone checkpoints, so the level is literally built from the player's own past two levels.

> DEVELOPER

maybe this part could be more of a matching game???

> AGENT

Yeah — that fixes the passivity. Instead of Enter → read → poof, make the poof earned:

**Walk into an anomaly and it shows you a *symptom*** — a glitched image or phrase, like a flickering hologram of vines, or sparking coils. The terminal opens with **three one-line answers from your history**:

```
what happened here?

  1 · Drone 02 rebuilt the nav core into a star dome
  2 · Drone 04 turned the air scrubbers into a garden pod
  3 · You salvaged the ignition coils from the crash site
```

**Pick the one that matches the symptom.** Vines flickering → the garden pod → press 2 → anomaly dissolves. Wrong pick → buzz, the anomaly stays, try again (maybe -5s on the clock so guessing isn't free).

So the loop becomes: spot → run → **match symptom to its history** → poof → next. Still one screen, still seconds per anomaly, but now there's a tiny "aha" in each one instead of a reading assignment — and it quietly proves the player *knows their own history*, which is the whole final-level point. The ten symptoms map to the ten things they actually did: five Level 1 salvages, five Level 2 upgrades.

The `entire search` / `entire explain` lines still flash by as the computer fetches the three candidates, so the skills lesson stays in frame without being the gameplay.

> DEVELOPER

its hard to match the symptom though like how would they know

> AGENT

Right — that's recall, and they only saw the garden pod once. Two fixes, both make the answer visible instead of remembered:

**Play the match with your feet.** Flip it: the anomaly *gives* you the history line — "Drone 04 turned the air scrubbers into a garden pod" — and you have to run to the thing it describes and touch it. The objects are all standing on the island already (your L1 salvage, your L2 upgrades), and the line literally names them. No memory needed, just read → spot → run. The matching becomes navigation, which is the part of these levels that's already fun, and the bird's-eye map turns into your answer board.

So: walk into anomaly → it hands you a line from your history → sprint to the thing it's talking about → touch it → both light up and the anomaly dissolves → next. Ten pairs, one clock.

**Or the lazier fix:** anomalies spawn *on* the objects themselves — the garden pod is right there glitching — so when the terminal shows three lines, the right one is just "the one naming the thing in front of you." Reading comprehension, not recall.

The first one is better gameplay (every anomaly becomes a little two-point sprint, the island is the puzzle), the second is simpler to build. Both keep one verb and zero typing.

> DEVELOPER

yeah im just like that description though is where its hard..how would you know that the air scrubber is now a garden pod

> AGENT

Because nothing on the island wears a name tag — yeah. The fix is just: give them name tags.

Every object gets a small floating label — `GARDEN POD`, `STAR DOME`, `PLASMA RING` — visible when you're near it, and all of them labeled on the bird's-eye map. Then the loop is airtight: anomaly hands you "Drone 04 turned the air scrubbers into a **garden pod**" → hit M → find `GARDEN POD` on the map → run → touch → poof. It's word-matching, not knowledge — the line names a thing, the world names its things, you connect them.

And honestly the labels aren't even a concession — they're the lesson wearing a costume. The whole point of checkpoints is that your work is *findable by name* instead of living in your memory. The island becomes a labeled history: ten things you did, each with a name and a story, and the level is just looking things up.

Even simpler variant if labels feel cluttered: only the bird's-eye map shows names (like a real map), ground level stays clean. M becomes the answer key, which finally makes the map mechanically necessary instead of just nice.

845	  // "Generate a dispatch summarizing recent agent work" — five drones just
846	  // worked; the report writes itself from their checkpoints.
847	  function sendDispatch() {
848	    reportSent = true;
849	    timerRunning = false;
850	    countdownEl?.classList.remove("is-low", "is-critical");
851	    if (termList) {
852	      termList.innerHTML =
853	        `<div class="term-list-row"><span class="tl-key tl-exp-key">DISPATCH</span>` +
854	        `<span class="tl-title">drone bay — day report</span></div>` +
855	        sites.map((s) =>
856	          `<div class="term-list-row"><span class="tl-key tl-exp-key">·</span>` +
857	          `<span class="tl-title">${s.sys.name.toLowerCase()} → ${s.sys.became} (${s.sys.ckpt.slice(0, 6)}…)</span></div>`
858	        ).join("") +
859	        `<div class="term-list-row"><span class="tl-key tl-exp-key">filed</span>` +
860	        `<span class="tl-title">from 5 checkpoints · crew: 1 human, 5 drones</span></div>`;
861	      termList.classList.remove("hidden");
862	    }
863	    flashTerminal("dispatch sent — look how much got done without you", true);
864	    renderTerminal();
865	    if (winSub) {
866	      winSub.textContent = "five jobs in parallel — the report wrote itself";
867	    }
868	    winTimer = setTimeout(() => {
869	      closeTerminal(false);
870	      tutorialEl?.classList.add("hidden");
871	      winEl?.classList.remove("hidden");
872	    }, 4000);
873	  }
874	  function submitCommand() {
875	    const n = normalizeCmd(buffer);
876	    buffer = "";
877	    if (!n) { renderTerminal(); return; }
878	    if (/^entire dispatch$/.test(n)) { sendDispatch(); return; }
879	    if (/^entire (checkpoint|cp) list$/.test(n)) {
880	      // L1's command still works — the raw log, then the nudge to report.
881	      if (termList) {
882	        termList.innerHTML = sites.map((s) =>
883	          `<div class="term-list-row"><span class="tl-id tl-id-short">${s.sys.ckpt}</span>` +
884	          `<span class="tl-title">drone fix: ${s.sys.name.toLowerCase()} → ${s.sys.became}</span></div>`
885	        ).join("");
886	        termList.classList.remove("hidden");
887	      }
888	      flashTerminal("the raw log — now turn it into the day's report:  entire dispatch", true);
889	      renderTerminal();

> AGENT

Done — every player-facing "drone" in Level 2 is now "subagent," and the build passes.

What changed:

- **Briefing**: story copy now introduces "five subagents, ready for orders," mission steps and controls say "send a subagent," the warning line, and the start button is **WAKE THE SUBAGENTS**
- **In-game prompts/tutorials**: "a subagent takes the job," "The subagent's got it," "A subagent finished while you were away," "Subagent at work," "use all five subagents," "The subagents kept working while you were in orbit"
- **Status panel**: working state reads `SUBAGENT AT WORK`
- **Terminal**: explain cards now open with `subagent · subagent-1` (etc.), the hint reads "# what did the subagent actually do?", `checkpoint list` rows say "subagent fix:", and the dispatch report files "crew: 1 human, 5 subagents"
- **Fail screen**: "Five subagents, one of you — use the fleet."

One judgment call: the *place* is still "The Drone Bay" (level title, "the ship's drone bay just woke up" hint) — the bay is where the subagents park, and it reads naturally next to the new copy. Say the word if you want the level renamed too (e.g., "The Subagent Bay" or something better). Code identifiers (`buildDrone`, `assignDrone`, etc.) stay as-is since they're not player-visible.

any other naming ideas..dont change anything just yet

> AGENT

A few directions, no changes made:

**Keep "The Drone Bay" with a fiction patch** — the drones are the *bodies*, the subagents are the minds piloting them. One line in the briefing ("five drones, each piloted by a subagent") makes the current name fully coherent. Cheapest option, and "drone bay" honestly sounds the most like a place on a ship.

**Rename to match the workers:**
- **The Fleet** — "FLEET ONLINE · LEVEL 2." The fail screen already says "use the fleet," so the vocabulary is in place. Strong, short.
- **The Hangar** — neutral ship-place that holds the fleet; doesn't name the workers at all, so it never goes stale.
- **The Agent Bay** — most literal swap; a little flat.

**Rename to match the lesson:**
- **Mission Control** — flips the framing to what *you* are in this level: the coordinator who assigns work and reviews it. Strongest thematically; slightly NASA instead of crashed-ship.
- **The Dispatch** — names the level after its finale command. Nice bow, but it spoils the ending and "dispatch" means the report, not the place.

My ranking: **The Fleet** if you want a rename, **fiction patch** if you want zero churn, **Mission Control** if you want the level named after the player's role rather than the workers. The rename touches maybe six strings (briefing tag/title, orbit hint, two comments, win-screen-adjacent copy), so any of these is a five-minute change.

> AGENT

That's exactly what the rename buys — with "drone" the level reads as sci-fi robots fixing a ship; with "subagent" the mapping to real life is impossible to miss: you fired off five agents, chose parallel vs. sequential yourself, and then used checkpoints to see what each one actually did.

The current copy already carries most of that weight: "too many to fix yourself," "watch one subagent work, or run five at once," the win line "five jobs in parallel — the report wrote itself," and the reveal "work you didn't watch is never a mystery." The insight half is the whole explain → ADD TO SHIP loop.

If you want to lean in further later, the two spots with room are the briefing story (it never says the word "parallel" — one sentence like "send them out one at a time or all at once — they work while you don't watch" would make the thesis explicit before play starts) and the win reveal (could name the pattern plainly: "that's subagents — work runs in parallel, checkpoints keep the receipts"). Both are one-line tweaks whenever you want them; I've noted the intent in memory and left everything as-is for now.
